| Shared accountability | Simultaneous answerability held by multiple people, roles, groups, or the organization for distinct or overlapping parts of a material decision, system, or outcome. | Must not become the claim that “everyone” is responsible while no duty, authority, or follow-up can be located. | Provisional | Values / Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Reasonable reliance | Reliance on an approved person, process, system, control, or specialist within one's role, training, authority, and available warning signs. | Permits specialization without blind reliance; material anomalies or contrary evidence may create verification or escalation duties. | Strong Candidate 03 boundary | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Systemic failure | A failure materially produced or enabled by interacting organizational conditions such as policy, authority, staffing, workload, training, incentives, process, technology, controls, or management decisions. | Does not automatically erase individual agency, misconduct, or duties to respond to warning signs. | Provisional | Governance / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Coherence | Sufficient alignment among knowledge, responsibilities, decisions, processes, learning, and tools for the organization to act intelligibly and consistently. | Coherence is not uniformity, rigidity, or suppression of legitimate disagreement. | Provisional | Manifesto / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Deliberate improvement | Intentional change grounded in evidence, experience, reasoning, challenge, continuity, and responsibility for consequences. | Does not mean constant change, automatic preservation of current practice, or acceptance of every explored idea. | Strong candidate | Manifesto / Principles / Glossary |
| Dependence | Reliance on instructions, people, systems, or controls to act or decide. | Dependence is not inherently harmful; the concern is brittle, permanent, or uncomprehending reliance inappropriate to the responsibility and risk. | Provisional | Manifesto / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Brittle dependence | Reliance that fails when instructions are incomplete, conditions change, exceptions arise, or the expected source is unavailable because underlying purpose and limits are not sufficiently understood. | Distinct from appropriate use of procedures, checklists, standards, workflows, supervision, and controls. | Strong candidate | Manifesto / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Client interest | The client's informed needs, priorities, constraints, and welfare as served through truthful, competent advice and meaningful choice. | Must not become paternalistic substitution of the advisor's preferred “long-term outcome” for the client's informed decision. | Strong Value candidate | Values / Glossary; implications for Canon |
| Sustainable capability | Organizational ability to perform responsibly and reliably without growth creating disproportionate fragility, errors, stress, or dependence on exceptional effort. | Must not imply that low volume or low reported problems automatically demonstrate competence. | Provisional | Values / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Structural fragility | An operating condition in which routine success depends materially on chronic overload, hidden effort, concentrated knowledge, deferred maintenance, weak controls, unowned exceptions, or systems unable to absorb foreseeable change or disruption. | Temporary strain, isolated error, or bounded experimentation does not by itself establish fragility. | Strong Candidate 04 failure concept | Values / Mental Models / Governance / Glossary |
| Operating margin | Practical capacity retained for exceptions, correction, learning, maintenance, adaptation, and disruption beyond expected routine demand. | Does not mean maximizing idle capacity or eliminating urgency; the required margin depends on consequence, volatility, and recoverability. | Provisional | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Responsible growth | Expansion of volume, scope, complexity, people, or technology whose risks, support needs, accountability, and effects on professional quality are proportionately understood and governed. | Growth may develop capability; it is not responsible merely because it is profitable, transparent, or supported by favorable metrics. | Strong Candidate 04 concept | Values / Principles / Glossary |
| Temporary strain | A limited, purposeful, monitored, supported, and recoverable period in which demand or difficulty exceeds normal operating conditions while contributing to a justified objective or learning. | Becomes structural fragility when repeatedly renewed, normalized, concealed, or unsupported. | Provisional | Principles / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Sufficient readiness | Preparation, competence, evidence, safeguards, authority, and response capacity proportionate to the foreseeable consequences of a proposed action. | Does not mean complete knowledge or perfect capability; its threshold rises with consequence, irreversibility, external obligation, and limited ability to detect or respond. | Strong Candidate 06 concept | Principles / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Bounded action | Action deliberately limited in scope, exposure, duration, dependency, or consequence so that learning or progress can occur without disproportionate risk. | A label such as pilot or experiment does not make action bounded; limits must be practical and account for cumulative and externalized effects. | Strong Candidate 06 concept | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Practical response capacity | Credible ability to detect, contain, pause, stop, correct, remediate, or redesign an action when material assumptions fail. | Broader and more realistic than theoretical rollback; some consequences cannot be erased even when operations can be reversed. | Strong Candidate 06 concept | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Cumulative exposure | Material risk or burden created by the interaction or repetition of individually limited actions. | Individually acceptable experiments may collectively create fragility, fatigue, complexity, or correlated failure. | Provisional Candidate 06 boundary | Mental Models / Governance / Glossary |
| Reconstructability | The degree to which future people can recover the consequential rationale, context, assumptions, evidence, authority, and dependencies needed to understand and evaluate past work. | Does not require reproducing lived experience, preserving every source, or treating reconstructed history as current authority. | Strong Candidate 07 concept | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Institutional intelligibility | The condition in which consequential organizational work remains understandable enough across time and personnel changes to be evaluated, maintained, challenged, or improved. | Documentation volume does not establish intelligibility; authority, currency, context, relationships, and material omissions still matter. | Provisional Candidate 07 concept | Principles / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Lossy synthesis | A summary or derived representation whose selection or compression omits context, evidence, uncertainty, dissent, provenance, or exceptions. | All synthesis selects; it becomes problematic when the loss is material to the intended decision, verification, reconstruction, or use. | Strong Candidate 07 boundary | Governance / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Active authority | The present repository status that makes a source controlling for its assigned knowledge type. | Historical preservation, provenance, accessibility, or prior authority does not by itself establish current authority. | Strong Candidate 07 distinction | Governance / Glossary |
| Sufficient clarity | Enough understanding of purpose, uncertainty, risk, next steps, and safeguards to proceed responsibly and learn further. | Neither perfect certainty nor permission for reckless implementation; threshold depends on stakes and reversibility. | Strong candidate concept | Principles / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Short-term advantage | An immediate benefit such as revenue, speed, convenience, scale, or reduced effort. | Not inherently bad; it becomes a trade-off concern when purchased by compromising a more durable commitment or obligation. | Provisional | Values / Glossary |
| Truthful advice | Competent advice that accurately represents the best available facts, uncertainty, material limitations, and professional reasoning without distortion for transactional advantage. | Truthfulness is non-negotiable, while the content and timing of advice remain contextual; it does not guarantee outcomes. | Strong Value candidate | Values / Glossary; implications for Canon |
| Client protection | Safeguarding a client's informed interests from avoidable harm through competent advice, disclosure, appropriate process, and responsible action. | Must not be justified by expected referrals or repeat business and must not become paternalistic control over client choice. | Strong Value candidate | Values / Principles / Glossary |
| Client autonomy | The client's authority to make an informed choice among lawful, available, and responsibly supportable options after receiving material information and professional interpretation. | Does not compel Spark to facilitate deception, illegality, unavailable options, action outside authority, or professionally indefensible conduct. | Strong candidate | Values / Principles / Glossary |
| Meaningful understanding | Sufficient practical comprehension of the material considerations, alternatives, uncertainties, limitations, and consequences needed to participate responsibly in a decision. | Spark can support and proportionately check understanding but cannot manufacture, perfectly observe, or guarantee another person's internal comprehension. | Strong Candidate 03 concept | Principles / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Paternalism | Substitution of a professional's or organization's preferred outcome for another person's lawful informed judgment on the assumption that the professional knows what is best. | Firm advice, refusal of indefensible conduct, and capacity safeguards are not automatically paternalistic; disagreement alone does not justify overriding agency. | Strong Candidate 03 failure concept | Principles / Glossary |
| Conflicting benefit | A personal, financial, transactional, organizational, relational, reputational, or convenience-based benefit capable of materially influencing professional judgment owed in a context. | Existence of benefit does not prove distortion; materiality, proximity, influence, safeguards, and external conflict rules remain relevant. | Strong Candidate 04 concept | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Independent professional judgment | Professional reasoning and recommendation formed and exercised without material distortion by an interest that conflicts with the judgment owed. | Does not require absence of compensation, relationships, emotion, or every interest; exact regulated duties and conflict standards remain externally constrained. | Strong Candidate 04 concept | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Practical authority | Authority that can realistically be exercised given actual information, access, competence, resources, time, organizational support, and freedom from improper obstruction or retaliation. | Formal authority may remain relevant but does not prove a person could meaningfully act in the circumstances. | Strong Candidate 05 concept | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Retained organizational accountability | Spark Mortgages' continuing answerability for operating conditions, systems, incentives, delegations, vendors, and authority structures it creates, approves, or continues to rely upon. | Does not make Spark the cause of every individual failure or erase real vendor and individual responsibility. | Strong Candidate 05 boundary | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Informed interests | The client's priorities, circumstances, constraints, risks, and legitimate objectives as understood through meaningful dialogue, material information, and competent professional interpretation. | Not identical to every stated preference, guaranteed outcome, advisor preference, or assumed long-term benefit. | Strong candidate | Values / Glossary; implications for Canon |
| Service boundary | A clearly communicated limit on the scope, time, expertise, availability, or work Spark can responsibly provide. | Must not be manipulated to favor revenue or used to conceal a material conflict; reasonable boundaries do not violate client interest. | Provisional | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Suitability | A professional and potentially regulated assessment of whether an option appropriately fits relevant client circumstances, objectives, risks, and obligations. | Exact meaning must be verified against applicable mortgage law, regulation, and professional standards before canonical use. | Provisional external term | Canon / Governance / Glossary |
| Professional judgment | Contextual evaluation and choice exercised using relevant knowledge, experience, evidence, values, authority, and accountability. | Explainability alone does not make judgment sound or authorize deviation from non-discretionary obligations. | Strong candidate | Principles / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Authorized deviation | A reasoned departure from a standard process made within delegated authority and applicable law, documented and escalated or reviewed according to risk. | Distinct from ignoring process because a person believes their judgment is superior. | Provisional | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Consequential decision | A choice or determination with material effects on clients, rights, obligations, risk, money, privacy, compliance, or organizational responsibility. | Boundary with bounded automated determinations remains unresolved; consequence varies by context and aggregation. | Provisional | Principles / AI Governance / Glossary |
| Knowledge compression | Proposed reduction or synthesis of accumulated material into more concise representations. | “Without loss” is generally unrealistic; compression may remove context, dissent, provenance, or edge cases and must not become a new repository lifecycle. | Discouraged term; prefer synthesis or curation | Governance / Glossary |
| Knowledge stewardship | Responsibility to preserve, maintain, challenge, retire, protect, and transmit institutional knowledge in forms appropriate to future use. | Does not require preserving everything and must respect privacy, legal retention, currency, retrieval cost, and historical value. | Strong Value candidate | Values / Governance / Glossary |
| Absent stakeholder | A person or group materially affected by a present decision but unable to participate in that decision or advocate for the relevant future interest. | For Candidate 05, the scope should remain tied to foreseeable future Spark roles and clients affected by continuity, not an unlimited duty to all future people. | Provisional | Values / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Responsible continuity | Deliberate preservation, transmission, challenge, maintenance, or retirement of consequential organizational knowledge and foundations so future work can proceed without reasonably avoidable loss. | Continuity is not permanence, exhaustive capture, guaranteed usefulness, or inherited authority. | Strong Candidate 05 concept | Values / Principles / Glossary |
| Responsible retirement | Deliberate removal from active authority, archival, restriction, expiration, or deletion of material whose continued use or retention is no longer justified. | Historical preservation and active authority are separate; law, privacy, security, evidence, and future consequence constrain the choice. | Strong Candidate 05 boundary | Governance / Glossary |
| Reconstructability | The practical ability to recover enough evidence, rationale, assumptions, provenance, status, and dissent to understand or challenge a consequential past decision. | Does not require keeping every draft or reproducing lived experience; required depth is proportionate to future consequence. | Provisional | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Irreducible commitment | The distinct non-negotiable refusal a candidate protects after examples, mechanisms, derivative rules, and overlapping evidence are removed. | Analytical test only; it is not a new canonical Value field or repository layer. | Working audit term | Workbench audit |
| Plain-language Value name | A memorable everyday name that identifies a Value's central tension or commitment without materially misleading when read alone. | Conversational appeal cannot substitute for precision; the card must clarify boundaries rather than reverse the apparent meaning of its name. | Working Distill standard | Values / Workbench audit |
| Canonical Values document | The single authoritative Philosophy document that defines Spark Mortgages' accepted Values and their relationship as a set. | Individual headings are addressable; separate per-Value files would require a demonstrated architectural need and must not create duplicate authority. | Recommended installation architecture | Values / Governance |
| Derivative Values summary | A shorter teaching, onboarding, interface, or quick-reference representation derived from the accepted canonical Values. | Must link to and remain subordinate to the canonical Values; it cannot silently simplify or redefine commitments. | Future optional artifact | Academy or justified downstream owner |
| Installation packet | A bounded set of implementation instructions generated from approved artifacts, decisions, mappings, status changes, and validation requirements. | Authorizes only the specified repository changes; it does not grant freedom to redraft approved Philosophy. | Proposed coordinated workflow artifact | Workbench / Governance process |
| Independent post-install audit | Verification of the implemented repository state against approved source text, installation instructions, authority boundaries, navigation, hashes, and validation results. | Strongest when performed outside the implementation task; it verifies installation rather than reopening approved substance without cause. | Proposed coordinated workflow step | Workbench audit / Governance process |
| Epistemic honesty | Honest representation of what is known, unknown, uncertain, inferred, disputed, stale, or corrected. | Requires useful explanation rather than merely saying “I don't know”; confidence and coherence must not exceed evidence. | Strong Value candidate | Values / Principles / Glossary |
| Material uncertainty | Uncertainty significant enough to affect a client's choice, risk assessment, recommendation, decision, or required oversight. | Not every unknown must be communicated with equal weight; relevance and consequence matter. | Provisional | Principles / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Materiality | The significance of a fact, omission, uncertainty, error, or limitation to reasonable understanding, decision-making, accountability, risk, or client interest in context. | Not identical to a legal materiality test unless an applicable law defines it; requires proportional judgment. | Strong candidate | Glossary / Principles / Governance |
| Reasonable verification | Proportionate effort to examine the evidence, sources, currency, and credible contrary information needed before making or preserving a material representation. | Does not require exhaustive investigation or perfect certainty; the required effort rises with consequence and available warning signs. | Strong candidate | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Willful blindness | Deliberately avoiding available information or verification to preserve deniability or a preferred belief. | Should not escape the Value merely because the resulting distortion was not directly confirmed as false. | Provisional | Values failure mode / Governance / Glossary |
| Material evidence | Evidence sufficiently relevant, credible, and consequential that it could reasonably affect a conclusion, decision, action, or required review. | Material does not mean decisive; disagreement, novelty, volume, or repetition alone do not establish material evidentiary weight. | Strong Principles Challenge term | Principles / Glossary / Governance |
| Credible influence risk | A reasonably supportable possibility that a benefit, incentive, relationship, pressure, or interest may materially affect professional investigation or judgment. | Triggers proportionate examination or safeguards but does not itself prove distortion or wrongdoing. | Strong Candidate 04 distinction | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Material distortion | A consequential alteration, omission, weighting, framing, or limitation that makes judgment or representation materially less faithful to its appropriate basis or purpose. | May arise intentionally, negligently, structurally, or through willful blindness; benefit or error alone does not prove it. | Provisional Candidate 04 term | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Reasonable ability | Practical capacity to act given relevant knowledge, competence, access, resources, time, support, opportunity, and foreseeable constraints. | Limited ability may reduce fair accountability but does not automatically erase duties to identify, pause, refuse, or escalate a known limitation. | Strong Candidate 05 term | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Authority status | The current relationship of a representation to accepted decision rights, including whether it is active, historical, superseded, proposed, restricted, or otherwise non-controlling. | Exact classifications remain Governance work; preserved content does not become current authority merely by remaining accessible. | Strong Candidate 07 distinction | Governance / Glossary |
| Traceability | The ability to reconstruct a representation's source, rationale, assumptions, decisions, changes, and supporting evidence to the degree required by its significance. | Does not require preserving or linking every intermediate artifact forever. | Strong candidate | Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| Adverse automated determination | A machine-produced classification, recommendation, or action that materially restricts access, removes an option, increases burden, or creates significant risk for a client or person. | Distinct from low-impact administrative automation; human review and recourse should be proportionate to consequences. | Provisional | AI Governance / Principles / Glossary |
| Metric integrity | Designing, interpreting, and revising measures so they reveal rather than conceal the underlying reality and do not reward gaming or underreporting. | A favorable number is not evidence of success when reporting behavior or case selection distorts it. | Strong candidate | Values / Principles / Governance / Glossary |
| External obligation | A legal, regulatory, court-ordered, contractual, or other binding requirement that constrains Spark's permissible actions. | Compliance does not imply agreement; disagreement must be challenged through lawful and authorized channels, not ignored in a client file. | Strong boundary | Governance / Glossary |
| Faithfulness to reality | A proposed commitment not to knowingly distort material facts, uncertainty, performance, responsibility, authority, or history for convenience, appearance, speed, or profit. | Does not require total disclosure, perfect objectivity, maximal detail, or violation of privacy/confidentiality; “reality” and materiality require practical definition. | Strong cross-cutting Value candidate | Values / Glossary |
| Mechanical compliance | Following a process or rule without exercising the understanding, judgment, responsibility, or escalation appropriate to its purpose and limits. | Distinct from mandatory compliance with external obligations and from deliberate use of essential safeguards. | Provisional | Principles / Mental Models / Glossary |
| Philosophical discovery | Identifying the non-negotiable commitment, cost, and refusal point revealed by a scenario. | Must occur before implementation allocation but does not automatically create a new Value. | Active Values method | Workbench only |
| Architectural allocation | Assigning a tested implication to its appropriate authoritative or implementation owner, such as Principles, Governance, AI Governance, Academy, Operations, or Canon. | Allocation must not replace philosophical discovery or imply all architecture derives only from Philosophy. | Active audit method | Workbench / Governance methodology |
| Extensible | Designed to allow justified additions as new evidence reveals genuinely distinct commitments. | Does not mean additive by default, endlessly expanding, or exempt from consolidation and retirement. | Strong working property | Values / Governance / Glossary |
| Durable Value | An accepted commitment expected to persist across changing tools and strategies while remaining open to deliberate correction, merger, or retirement. | Durable does not mean immutable, perfectly worded, or insulated from evidence and organizational change. | Strong candidate | Values / Governance / Glossary |
| Cost boundary | The meaningful revenue, speed, convenience, appearance, scale, effort, or opportunity Spark is willing to sacrifice rather than compromise a Value. | Corresponds to the repository's existing Trade-off field and should not create a competing Value schema. | Active audit term | Values / Glossary |
| Value conflict | A situation in which honoring one accepted commitment appears to constrain or compromise another. | Cannot always be solved by ranking; applicable obligations, Principles, authority, context, and explicit decision may be required. | Provisional | Values / Principles / Glossary |
| Set-level closure | A Workbench decision that the current candidate inventory has been sufficiently compared and bounded for its present purpose. | It stops further conceptual cycling but does not accept, reject, distill, install, or make the candidates canonical. | Active Mental Models method | Workbench only |
| Continue observation before Distill | A candidate disposition used when a concept remains plausible and bounded but lacks repeated practical evidence sufficient for canonical drafting. | Observation does not require formal tracking or validation infrastructure; later experience may support advancement, narrowing, reclassification, or rejection. | Active Mental Models disposition | Workbench only |
| Lineage bias | The risk that an exploration's candidate set reflects the ideas and examples that initiated the inquiry more strongly than the organization's actual recurring reasoning needs. | It does not justify manufacturing balancing candidates; future comparison should use evidence from real recurring decisions. | Active audit caution | Workbench audit |
| Reclassification risk | The possibility that a useful candidate belongs in a downstream practice, standard, or owner rather than in the canonical layer where it is being explored. | Usefulness alone does not establish Philosophy ownership; recurring activation and distinct contribution must be demonstrated. | Active audit caution | Workbench audit / future destination owner |
| Prototype review packet | A bounded non-authoritative set containing an artifact, its status and scope, its source map, and the common questions reviewers should apply. | It creates a shared review object without granting authority or requiring a new review system. | Active Academy prototype method | Workbench |
| Frozen review revision | A specific repository commit or exact exported file set that every reviewer examines before agreed revisions are applied. | It prevents different reviewers from unknowingly evaluating different versions; it does not imply immutability or acceptance. | Active cross-platform review method | Workbench / Git workflow |
| Source-provenance mismatch | A condition in which an artifact's nearby repository files or recorded base differ from the sources actually consulted during authoring. | The content may still be aligned, but reviewers need the actual source revision to reconstruct and verify that alignment. | Active audit finding | Workbench / Governance provenance |
| External-only prototype surface | A prototype interface or artifact that exists only inside a private tool and lacks a versioned repository representation. | It may support exploration but cannot serve as a durable, independently reviewable dependency until faithfully exported or otherwise preserved. | Active prototype limitation | Workbench / future Academy implementation |
| Preservation commit | A specific repository revision that freezes non-authoritative source material and its review context so multiple reviewers can examine the same artifact. | Preservation establishes traceability, not accuracy, acceptance, authority, or readiness to merge. | Active cross-platform review term | Workbench / Git workflow |
| Author-asserted correspondence | A claimed relationship between two artifact surfaces based on the author's process or records when an independent comparison was not possible. | It may support provenance but must not be described as independently verified or as an automated version lock. | Active prototype provenance term | Workbench / future implementation governance |
| Derived instructional claim | A proposition presented in an example, rationale, answer key, feedback item, or assessment because it follows from source teaching material. | Fictional case facts do not make the derived answer fictional; its accuracy and authority still depend on the underlying domain claim. | Strong prototype-audit distinction | Academy / Canon / Glossary if later needed |
| Learner-facing authority gradient | The tendency for polished explanations, answer keys, feedback, or interface elements to feel more settled than earlier caveats or source-status warnings. | Opening disclaimers do not neutralize later categorical instruction; unsupported content should remain visibly bounded at the point of use. | Provisional Academy audit concept | Academy / Workbench |
| Instructional taxonomy | A defined set of categories and distinctions used to organize what learners are taught and how cases are classified. | It must state what it classifies—law, insurer eligibility, lender treatment, operational workflow, or another scope—before examples and answers can be evaluated reliably. | Strong course-prototype finding | Canon first; Academy representation |
| Scope contradiction | Evidence that a broad or apparently universal statement fails in a material subset because its intended domain was not stated narrowly enough. | It may invalidate the wording without proving that every narrower case is wrong. | Active audit distinction | Canon / Academy / Glossary if later needed |

## Audit Register

| ID | Label | Finding | Effect | Recommended handling |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| A-001 | Watch | A second working-memory system could duplicate Spark Brain or Design History. | Creates competing continuity or authority layers. | Keep this as one non-authoritative Workbench file; do not add it to Brain or Design History during drafting. |
| A-002 | Clear | The requested audit role matches the repository's existing emphasis on coherence, deliberate acceptance, and resisting premature complexity. | Strengthens review without changing authority. | Continue with source-preserving audit entries and explicit acceptance gates. |
| A-003 | Watch | “All responses” can create a large transcript with low retrieval value. | Important decisions may become harder to find. | Preserve submissions faithfully, but maintain a compact synthesis and only recommend archival history when the conversation has enduring architectural value. |
| A-004 | Clear | The actual Spark Manifesto is organizational rather than a complete Spark OS philosophy. | Clarifies that it should not simply replace the repository's Spark OS Manifesto placeholder. | Preserve it with minimal wording changes while deciding its correct authority and location. |
| A-005 | Decision needed | The browser proposal redefines Spark OS from a knowledge system for Spark Mortgages into a general operating philosophy for competent people, trustworthy organizations, and knowledge systems. | Materially expands product and organizational scope. | Do not adopt through prose alone; Sheldon must explicitly choose the intended scope and downstream implications. |
| A-006 | Drift | The eleven-document “one book” structure replaces the accepted five-document Philosophy framework without reviewing current repository architecture. | Creates overlapping authorities, deeper sequencing, and more maintenance. | Draft into the existing five boundaries first; add a document only when content demonstrates a unique, durable purpose. |
| A-007 | Conflict | Moving the Manifesto to the final chapter contradicts `01-philosophy/README.md` and `01-philosophy/manifesto.md`, which make it the starting point. | Reverses the accepted Philosophy dependency order and affects navigation and downstream drafting. | Treat as an architectural proposal requiring deliberate review and likely an ADR if accepted. |
| A-008 | Watch | Calling Philosophy “one book” privileges linear reading over modular retrieval and reuse. | May weaken Spark OS as an operating knowledge system and encourage repeated cross-chapter exposition. | Allow narrative progression, but keep each canonical document independently purposeful and linkable. |
| A-009 | Clear | “Systems are built by respecting dependencies” is a strong candidate principle or mental model grounded in recurring Manifesto language. | Could unify learning, competence, trust, responsibility, and autonomy without adding a new architecture. | Test each dependency individually and place the accepted model in Principles or Mental Models. |
| A-010 | Watch | Several proposed dependencies extend beyond the source Manifesto: responsibility before authority, authority before autonomy, and knowledge before understanding. | Polished symmetry can make unproven claims appear established. | Mark them as hypotheses and define each term before accepting the chain. |
| A-011 | Conflict | “The ultimate purpose ... is not to build a mortgage brokerage” sits uneasily with the root definition of Spark OS as the knowledge operating system for Spark Mortgages. | Could detach the system from its concrete institutional purpose. | Reframe as implementation-versus-purpose only if Spark Mortgages remains the intended organizational context. |
| A-012 | Watch | Epistemology, one-source-of-truth governance, AI collaboration, and decision frameworks are bundled into Philosophy. | Risks moving existing Governance decisions into a competing philosophical layer. | Keep philosophical premises in Philosophy and operational authority rules in Governance/ADRs, linked rather than duplicated. |
| A-013 | Clear | The “Purpose” draft contains useful candidate language about turning information into understanding, competence, judgment, and trustworthy action. | Provides substantive input for the existing Spark OS Manifesto. | Edit for scope, factual humility, and alignment before considering acceptance. |
| A-014 | Clear | The latest exchange explicitly distinguishes exploratory granularity from canonical simplicity. | Corrects the assumption that every important idea deserves a permanent top-level document. | Preserve this distinction as a working practice. |

| A-403 | Watch | The exploration risks turning the Principle into a complete risk-management framework. | Likelihood, consequence, reversibility, detectability, safeguards, monitoring, stop authority, cumulative exposure, and debt all appear in detail. | Preserve the tests as source evidence but distill one durable rule; allocate operational thresholds and controls downstream. |
| A-404 | Clear | Temporary strain versus structural fragility is a useful supporting distinction but not a second Principle. | It helps diagnose whether bounded difficulty is becoming the operating model but does not independently guide the full proceed-or-delay decision. | Carry it as a Mental Model candidate without accepting its final destination yet. |
| A-405 | Clear | Anti-stagnation is a necessary gaming boundary rather than a standalone Principle. | Separate anti-stagnation language would require the proportionality and fragility boundaries to avoid becoming growth ideology. | Keep within Candidate 06 and reject a separate Principle for now. |
| A-406 | Clear | Candidate 07 is the correct next exploration. | The final provisional family concerns preserving enough rationale, provenance, context, and source material for future people to understand, challenge, reconstruct, and improve consequential work. | Proceed without substantive Sheldon input. |
| A-407 | Clear | Candidate 07 is independently Principle-worthy when centered on future intelligibility rather than recordkeeping. | It changes recurring decisions about what must survive consequential work, what present cost is justified, and when dependence on an original decision-maker is unacceptable. | Retain for complete-set comparison. |
| A-408 | Refine | `Preserve the Ability to Understand and Improve` may imply that Spark can preserve another person's understanding or guarantee improvement. | Spark can preserve the conditions and representations needed for future understanding and improvement, not the human capability or outcome itself. | Test names such as `Keep Consequential Work Understandable` or `Preserve What Future Judgment Requires`. |
| A-409 | Clear | Candidate 07 remains distinct from Candidate 01 despite close temporal dependence. | Candidate 01 governs clarity about the basis and limits of judgment when expressed; Candidate 07 governs whether enough of that basis survives time, transition, compression, and retirement. | Preserve both through set comparison and test whether their canonical wording avoids duplication. |
| A-410 | Clear | Preservation and active authority must remain separate. | Historical, superseded, or restricted material may be necessary for reconstruction while being unsafe as current guidance. | Preserve the distinction as a boundary; leave classifications and controls to Governance. |
| A-411 | Clear | The candidate properly rejects indiscriminate preservation. | Privacy, deletion duties, security, cognitive load, obsolescence, and low reconstruction cost can justify restriction, retirement, compression, or loss. | Do not turn the Principle into a universal retention command. |
| A-412 | Refine | `Unnecessary dependence` requires proportional interpretation. | Temporary reliance on experts, specialization, or tacit judgment may be legitimate; the concern is material continuity risk that proportionate preservation could reasonably reduce. | Preserve the key-person boundary without implying that every dependency is a violation. |
| A-413 | Watch | Several proposed Mental Models are plausible but not yet earned. | Institutional Memory Is Not Record Volume and Historical Preservation Is Not Current Authority may be durable distinctions; Preservation Cost vs Relearning Cost may be only a trade-off test. | Carry them as signals into Mental Models exploration without accepting or multiplying them now. |
| A-414 | Clear | The four internal signals form one Principle plus boundaries and downstream controls. | Separate preservation, evidence-retention, retirement, and anti-relearning Principles would overconstitutionalize knowledge management. | Retain one Principle candidate only. |

| A-417 | Clear | ADR-0003's Review Trigger remains within scope. | Its triggers concern parser reliability, authority-path representation, and retirement or replacement of the compared Brain representations; it does not prescribe Canon taxonomy. | No revision required for scope creep. |

| A-430 | Refine | Candidate 07's `improvable` wording is awkward and may imply a guaranteed outcome. | Spark preserves conditions future people can use; it cannot ensure that they understand, maintain, challenge, or improve the work. | Prefer language about enabling or supporting future judgment without weakening the preservation duty. |

## Decision Register

| ID | Status | Decision | Basis |
|---|---|---|---|
| D-001 | Working convention | Store this audit in `00-workbench/drafts/` as one non-authoritative file. | It is active exploratory material and must not compete with Spark Brain or accepted knowledge. |
| D-002 | Working convention | Separate verbatim source entries from Codex synthesis and findings. | Preserves provenance and prevents interpretation from being mistaken for source content. |
| D-003 | Pending | Determine whether Spark and Spark OS require separate manifestos and where the organizational Manifesto belongs. | The supplied document is organizational, but the repository currently defines only a Spark OS Philosophy Manifesto. |
| D-004 | Pending | Confirm or reject the browser conversation's broader definition of Spark OS. | This choice constrains all later Philosophy documents and cannot be inferred from stylistic approval. |
| D-005 | Pending | Retain or revise the five-document Philosophy framework. | The proposed eleven-document book is a material architectural change with substantial overlap. |
| D-006 | Working convention | Explore the expanded concepts without treating the expanded document list as repository architecture. | Sheldon wants full creative exploration plus a short-term consolidation roadmap. |
| D-007 | Working convention | Use the original five Philosophy documents as the current consolidation target. | No additional canonical document has yet demonstrated a unique, non-overlapping purpose. |
| D-008 | Working convention | Track concept-to-document mappings in this audit rather than creating a Philosophy roadmap or Thought Paper subsystem. | Existing Workbench and Philosophy README already provide the necessary lifecycle and stable map. |
| D-009 | Working convention | Treat “Thought Papers and Knowledge Distillation” as a loose Workbench drafting guide supplied for audit, not an accepted Spark OS methodology. | The document contains useful heuristics alongside unresolved governance conflicts and universal claims. |
| D-010 | Working convention | Do not add a Design History record for this exchange now. | The method has not yet been accepted, installed, reused, or shown to shape enduring architecture. |
| D-011 | Working convention | Adopt continuous five-document mapping during exploration without altering the five documents' accepted roles. | The method now supports the repository architecture, but the browser response's shorthand definitions do not fully match it. |
| D-012 | Working convention | Require a primary canonical owner for each concept during consolidation. | Exploratory mapping may identify several consequences, but installation must avoid duplicate definitions. |
| D-013 | Working convention | Keep the current Knowledge definition and ladder provisional. | Both require boundary testing against understanding, judgment, competence, tacit knowledge, and institutional knowledge. |
| D-014 | Working convention | Accept the cost-and-trade-off definition of Values as the leading formulation, subject to final wording and Sheldon’s approval. | It aligns with the existing Value design and acceptance standards. |
| D-015 | Working convention | Treat application as evidence for competence and usefulness, not a universal truth-validation method. | The browser response explicitly accepts this distinction. |
| D-016 | Working convention | Distinguish the repository from Spark OS: repository as authoritative knowledge substrate; Spark OS as the broader system that supports preservation, learning, judgment, and capability. | This preserves both the Markdown source-of-truth decision and the system's broader purpose. |
| D-017 | Working convention | Maintain a live provisional glossary inside this audit after every supplied exchange. | Emerging terminology must be tracked and reconciled without becoming a competing canonical Glossary. |
| D-018 | Working convention | Retain the four-question Knowledge/Understanding/Judgment/Competence model as a provisional boundary test, not an accepted ontology. | It reduces overlap but leaves truth, justification, representation, and capability questions unresolved. |
| D-019 | Working convention | Use Builder, Auditor, and Distiller only as informal modes. | The pattern may help the present workflow but has not justified new roles or governance. |
| D-020 | Working convention | Keep ontology work proportionate to the five-document Philosophy task. | Shared language enables the other documents but does not replace or precede them as a new foundation. |
| D-021 | Working convention | Close active expansion of Exploration 001 after the completed edge-case test. | The model now has sufficient distinctions, limitations, mapped implications, and unresolved notes to inform later drafting without further prerequisite work. |
| D-022 | Working convention | Reject the Knowledge Ladder and retain an iterative four-concept relationship. | The stress tests show no universal acquisition sequence or guarantee among Knowledge, Understanding, Judgment, and Competence. |
| D-023 | Working convention | Park the Epistemic Status Model for possible Governance review. | Its current categories mix authority, evidence, lifecycle, currency, and historical state and should not be installed from Philosophy exploration. |
| D-024 | Working convention | Advance the humility boundaries as strong candidates for Manifesto and Principles. | They survived misinformation, stale-fact, tacit-expertise, disputed-claim, and opaque-AI tests. |
| D-025 | Working convention | Accept Exploration 001 as closed working source material. | The browser response has now explicitly adopted the audit's closeout recommendation and selective five-document mapping. |
| D-026 | Working convention | Begin actual Manifesto drafting without further prerequisite exploration. | The current outline is sufficient to proceed while retaining unresolved scope and wording cautions. |
| D-027 | Working convention | Fix the current Spark OS Philosophy scope on Spark Mortgages. | Sheldon explicitly endorses the repository's active purpose and defers generalized abstraction until later evidence justifies it. |
| D-028 | Working convention | Add Close to the exploratory workflow as a source-readiness gate. | Exploration 001 demonstrated the need to stop once material is sufficiently challenged and mapped. |
| D-029 | Working convention | Refer to browser-produced foundational prose as candidate drafts until explicit acceptance and repository installation. | Conversation quality or completion does not establish canonical authority. |
| D-030 | Installation requirement | Preserve Explore → Challenge → Audit → Close → Distill as a durable working method available during Full Architectural Boot. | Sheldon explicitly requests that future boot behavior pick up this fundamental work style. |
| D-031 | Pending architectural choice | Satisfy the boot requirement through an existing Governance/Contribution source already loaded by Full Architectural Boot when possible. | Avoids copying substantive methodology into Brain; any actual boot behavior change must be reviewed under the Brain Constitution and ADR criteria. |

| D-035 | Working convention | Close the Manifesto revision cycle and mark the final candidate acceptance-ready. | All material audit findings have been resolved; remaining notes are optional line edits rather than conceptual blockers. |

| D-037 | Authority clarification | Disregard the browser assistant's acceptance declaration as an authoritative decision. | AI may draft, review, and recommend but may not decide Spark Philosophy or Canon. |
| D-038 | Proposed lifecycle | Use Candidate → Approved for installation → Accepted/Active after installation rather than “Accepted Canonical Candidate.” | Separates human approval, repository action, and current authority. |
| D-039 | Installation mapping | Install the approved candidate into `01-philosophy/manifesto.md` during the coordinated foundational installation, with required navigation, state, validation, and downstream review. | The approved artifact is preserved at `00-workbench/drafts/spark-os-manifesto-approved-candidate.md`. |
| D-040 | Working convention | Advance Values Exploration 001 from Explore to Challenge. | The first five trade-offs produced credible candidate commitments and several unresolved boundaries. |
| D-041 | Working convention | Do not promote the $1M problem ratio or other operational measures into Philosophy. | Metrics are evidence and implementation tools whose validity requires separate operational design. |
| D-042 | Working convention | Keep “long-term capability over short-term advantage” as a candidate pattern, not an accepted umbrella Value. | It is promising but currently overgeneralizes five distinct trade-offs. |
| D-043 | Working convention | Advance truthful, competent advice serving client interest as the strongest current Value candidate. | It survives absence of future revenue, referrals, reputation benefit, or strategic advantage. |
| D-044 | Working convention | Treat professional-judgment-over-process as a candidate Principle, not a Value. | It operationalizes commitments and requires authority, legal, risk, documentation, and escalation boundaries. |
| D-045 | Working convention | Reject the proposed linear knowledge-compression lifecycle as repository architecture. | It duplicates existing lifecycle Governance and mixes incomparable concepts. |
| D-046 | Working convention | Keep human-decision exclusivity unresolved pending a consequential-decision taxonomy. | The underlying accountability commitment is strong, but the universal wording is not implementable. |
| D-047 | Boundary result | Treat truthful advice under uncertainty as epistemic honesty plus competent client guidance. | Honest uncertainty must be paired with evidence, implications, and client-relevant reasoning. |
| D-048 | Boundary result | Allow process deviation only within delegated authority and non-discretionary constraints, with documentation and review proportionate to risk. | The test case supports judgment without process worship. |
| D-049 | Boundary result | Treat silent automated removal of viable client options as consequential and unacceptable without accountable review and recourse. | False-negative harm outweighs pure throughput efficiency in the tested case. |
| D-050 | Boundary result | Require proportional traceability for material canonical guidance rather than universal source retention. | Summarization loses information, but preserving everything would recreate fragility and overload. |
| D-051 | Boundary result | Prefer transparent reporting and learning over favorable metrics produced by concealment. | Metric integrity is evidence of accountability and epistemic honesty. |
| D-052 | Boundary result | External obligations constrain capability and client-interest choices; disagreement must use authorized channels. | Legal and regulatory compliance cannot be overridden through Spark-specific professional judgment. |
| D-053 | Method correction | Require Value-first boundary analysis before assigning downstream Principles or repository ownership. | Sheldon correctly identifies that implementation classification occasionally overshadowed philosophical discovery. |
| D-054 | Working convention | Record two analytical outputs in the existing audit—philosophical discovery and architectural allocation—without creating separate artifacts. | Preserves simplicity and avoids a new document layer. |
| D-055 | Working convention | Add cost/refusal point to the browser's four-question boundary method. | A Value is meaningful only when Spark will bear a real cost rather than compromise it. |
| D-056 | Working convention | Keep “faithfulness to reality” as a strong cross-cutting candidate pending a focused limits test. | It may unify several signals but must survive confidentiality, simplification, uncertainty, and materiality cases. |
| D-057 | Phase transition | Move Values Exploration 001 from Challenge into Audit. | The candidate families have survived trade-off exploration, boundary testing, and method correction. |
| D-058 | Working convention | Audit five candidate families without assuming they become five accepted Values. | Audit may merge, refine, reject, retain, or reopen a candidate. |
| D-059 | Working convention | Use the existing seven-part Value design standard for candidates and the eventual document. | Avoids a competing four-part schema and preserves downstream traceability. |
| D-060 | Working convention | Treat the Values document as extensible but not additive by default. | A new Value requires a demonstrated gap and may trigger consolidation or revision of existing Values. |
| D-061 | Candidate disposition | Candidate 01 is Refine, not yet Retain. | The substantive commitment survives, but its name and knowing-only failure standard require revision before Distill. |
| D-062 | Working convention | Preserve the full Candidate 01 evidence in the audit while planning a materially shorter final Value entry. | Audit depth should not determine canonical document length. |
| D-063 | Candidate disposition | Candidate 02 is Refine. | The commitment passes, but its name, equal-service interpretation, and external-option boundary need complete-set refinement. |

| D-065 | Pre-audit framing | Interpret Candidate 03 as preventing accountability from disappearing, not as prohibiting delegation. | Work, authority, and bounded execution can be delegated while accountable ownership remains explicit. |

## Parking Lot

- A qualifying Design History summary after the foundational architecture is accepted, but only if the conversation materially shapes long-term architecture.
- A final installation prompt generated from accepted decisions, document mappings, unresolved items, and repository verification requirements.
- A possible standalone Epistemology document, reconsidered only if drafting proves the concept cannot be clearly housed in the existing framework.
- A possible organizational area for Spark-specific material, reconsidered only after repository scope and authority are explicitly decided.
- The eleven-document outline as a future review lens, not a current installation plan.
- A Thought Paper methodology, reconsidered only if repeated use demonstrates a gap in the existing Workbench workflow.

## Source Ledger

### S-001 — 2026-07-21 — Sheldon Phillips

**Context:** Initial request defining the audit and working-memory role.

> I am working with ChatGPT in a browser chat to write the foundational documents for Spark OS. AS the working memory can get a little eclectic and I myself am only human, I want you to act as a audit and secondary check to the structure of the conversation and make sure we are keeping on track, reviewing and flagging any over-complication or feature creep like thought patterns.
>
> Please keep a local working memory of all the responses I post inside of this chat and structure the overarching conversation, concepts and documents for later installation into the Spark-OS github. I will be requesting the prompt from this chat in order to properly install the foundational documents into Spark-OS.

**Audit note:** This establishes process and scope, not foundational Spark OS content. The request is compatible with the existing Workbench lifecycle as long as this audit remains non-authoritative and separate from Spark Brain.

### S-002 — 2026-07-21 — Browser conversation supplied by Sheldon Phillips

**Context:** Review of `00 The Spark Manifesto.docx`, followed by a proposed Philosophy architecture and a draft chapter titled “01 — Purpose.”

**Verbatim source:** Codex attachment `pasted-text.txt` (local attachment path intentionally omitted)
**SHA-256:** `D10F90574EA82731FF007AC86D7ED45DD680630DFDC32742E0D83556B610C534`

**Conversation sequence:**

1. The browser assistant classifies the uploaded Manifesto as organizational Spark philosophy rather than Spark OS philosophy.
2. It proposes that Spark OS Philosophy should sit upstream of the operating system, organization, and organizational Manifesto.
3. It identifies ordered dependencies—preparation, demonstrated ability, competence, confidence, trust, responsibility, authority, and autonomy—as a potential unifying systems philosophy.
4. Sheldon authorizes proceeding with the previously outlined documents.
5. The browser assistant expands the Philosophy structure to eleven documents, adding Epistemology and several other layers.
6. It reframes Spark OS as an operating philosophy rather than primarily a software project and proposes treating the documents as chapters in one book.
7. Sheldon authorizes beginning.
8. The browser assistant moves the Manifesto to the end and drafts “01 — Purpose,” presenting Spark OS as a system for transforming information into understanding, competence, judgment, and trustworthy action.

**Document evidence checked:** The actual Manifesto repeatedly refers to Spark, mortgage advisors, clients, referral partners, lenders, brokerage standards, professional development, and Spark's legacy. It explicitly states “Preparation must come before Responsibility” and “Demonstrated Ability must come before Trust,” and describes competence as instilling confidence and confidence as earning trust.

**Audit note:** The core organizational-versus-system distinction is credible, and the dependency theme is promising. The subsequent eleven-document architecture, book model, Manifesto reversal, and generalized scope are proposals—not accepted consequences of that observation. They conflict with active repository boundaries and require explicit decisions before further drafting hardens them into assumed architecture.

### S-003 — 2026-07-21 — Sheldon Phillips and browser conversation

**Sheldon's prompt to the browser chat:**

> First, I love that we are expanding and becoming more granular and nuanced along the way.
>
> However, we may be drifting into a bit of over complexity with our separation from the original 5 philosophy documents.
>
> I do not want to restrict your creativity or decision making so please continue with what we have discussed most recently BUT also reconcile in the documents that follow this response, where they would land in the original 5 foundation documents. I want to flesh out all the ideas as suggested but also provide a road map to consolidate the working version in the short term while also giving us the ability to review the original expanded 11 documents in the future once we have further elaborated on the project.

**Browser response sequence:**

1. It recognizes the eleven-document expansion as premature permanent architecture and proposes optimizing for current rather than eventual complexity.
2. It distinguishes a granular Working Architecture from a minimal Canonical Architecture.
3. It proposes unconstrained “Thought Papers,” possibly twenty or thirty, for concepts such as knowledge, competence, judgment, trust, authority, systems, and continuous improvement.
4. It proposes a flow from Idea → Thought Paper → Emergent Patterns → Canonical Philosophy → Operating Principles → Implementation.
5. It suggests making this a general Spark OS knowledge-creation methodology.
6. It proposes `01-philosophy/00-philosophy-roadmap.md` to bridge the Thought Papers and five canonical Philosophy documents.

**Audit note:** The distinction between exploration and canonical presentation is correct and already supported by Spark OS. The proposed Thought Paper subsystem and Philosophy roadmap are not needed to realize it. Existing Workbench drafts can hold exploration, the current Philosophy README can retain the stable target architecture, and this audit can map each explored idea to its likely canonical destination. The response has therefore found the right principle but proposed more machinery than the present system requires.

### S-004 — 2026-07-21 — Sheldon Phillips, browser conversation, and methodology document

**Sheldon's prompt to the browser chat:**

> Thought papers are essentially what we have built as a workbench inside the github, apologies your access remains a little limited for some reason but it does also let us work with some freedom that the system may not allow. So good and bad.
>
> I do like the concept of this being a philosophy going forward that Spark OS works on ideas and concepts more broadly before refining it all into what we had envisioned as the end goal. A sort of “begin with the end in mind” methodology that does not limit initial brainstorming and creativity.
>
> 1. Please Create a markdown file that fleshes out that methodology loosely. I will add it as a source to my codex chat in which I am auditing ourselves along the way and will generate the installation prompt within that chat later.
>
> 2. Refocus back on track with the write up of the ideas exploration we will refine into the 5 foundational documents.

**Browser response sequence:**

1. It recommends possible later Design History preservation and accepts that the five documents are the destination rather than the starting point.
2. It drafts “Thought Papers and Knowledge Distillation,” placed conceptually in the Workbench.
3. The method distinguishes exploratory thinking from distilled knowledge and proposes: Observe → Question → Explore → Challenge → Connect → Distill → Validate → Simplify → Repeat.
4. It states that Thought Papers may challenge, contradict, split, and merge, while canonical Philosophy should contain enduring material.
5. It then proposes that “reality has an order,” elevates dependencies into a possible metaphysical premise, and replaces Purpose as the starting exploration with `TP-001 — Order` followed by Knowledge, Understanding, Competence, and Trust.

**Supplied methodology document:** `Thought Papers and Knowledge Distillation.docx` (local source path intentionally omitted)
**Document SHA-256:** `F2BAD34BD6B4BFD73BA1137E5EC2E42CDC9854615EEA090B7DE4D709D54AC53D`
**Pasted exchange SHA-256:** `B4F2420F4F646BA8EC63FD3273907C3391930FCEA30211C9B48083215308437A`

**Document evidence checked:** The Word document contains 76 paragraphs, one section, no tables, and no actual comments despite including a comments part. It defines the exploratory/distillation method but also claims canonical statements should remain valid regardless of industry and that irreducible concepts become Philosophy. Page layout could not be verified because explicit page-size metadata is absent and LibreOffice is unavailable.

**Audit note:** Sheldon has resolved the structural question correctly: Thought Papers are a possible form of Workbench exploration, not another repository layer. The methodology is useful as loose source material, especially its distinction between clarity and compression. It is not ready to become governance because it overstates unconstrained exploration, automatic preservation, cross-industry universality, and simplification as an acceptance test. The proposed “Order” thesis should be explored as a candidate Principle or Mental Model, not assumed as the first truth from which the rest must follow.

### S-005 — 2026-07-21 — Sheldon Phillips and browser conversation

**Sheldon's prompt to the browser chat:**

> Keep “Order” as a candidate principle or mental model, not the assumed foundation of reality. Begin substantive exploration now, and map each concept continuously into Manifesto, Values, Principles, Mental Models, and Glossary. Do not introduce another prerequisite layer before writing those foundations.

**Browser response sequence:**

1. It accepts that Order was being derived before it had earned foundational status.
2. It proposes the rule: “Nothing becomes foundational because it appears elegant. It becomes foundational because it repeatedly survives exploration.”
3. It adopts continuous mapping into the original five documents.
4. It begins `Exploration 001 — Knowledge` and distinguishes documented information from knowledge understood sufficiently for appropriate use.
5. It proposes characteristics of knowledge, including contextuality, support for prediction and judgment, growth through application, deterioration without challenge, and transfer through understanding.
6. It preserves open questions about explanation, experience, understanding, organizational knowledge, and AI.
7. It maps candidate consequences into Manifesto, Values, Principles, Mental Models, and Glossary, including a provisional Knowledge Ladder.

**Audit note:** The process correction is successful: exploration is now substantive, provisional, and connected to the five-document destination. The next risk is semantic collapse. The draft currently makes Knowledge contain understanding and reliable action, while also placing Understanding, Judgment, and Competence after it in a ladder. It also slightly rewrites the accepted roles of Values and Principles. The best next move is not another architecture discussion; it is to test the proposed distinctions against concrete cases and counterexamples, then revise the definition and model.

### S-006 — 2026-07-21 — Sheldon Phillips and browser conversation

**Sheldon's prompt to the browser chat:**

> Looks like progress! Here are my Key corrections:
>
> - Values should answer “What does Spark refuse to compromise?” Behaviours and qualities are evidence or consequences of those commitments.
> - Principles are durable decision rules derived from Values—not a container for every proposed “enduring truth.”
> - “Knowledge cannot be stored” conflicts with Spark OS as a canonical knowledge system if taken literally. A better distinction may be between stored knowledge representations and knowledge embodied in human capability.
> - The definition currently absorbs understanding, judgment, and competence into Knowledge, then places those concepts after Knowledge in a ladder.
> - The proposed “Knowledge Ladder” omits Knowledge itself.
> - Application may validate competence, but it does not necessarily establish whether information is true.
> - Curiosity, continuous learning, and intellectual honesty remain candidate Values until each demonstrates meaningful trade-offs and non-negotiable commitment.
>
> Then after review of the above corrections:
>
> 1. What is factual vs procedural knowledge?
> 2. Does knowledge count or exist if there is no immediate application?
> 3. Is this repository for example, a knowledge base or an information base?

**Pasted exchange SHA-256:** `3510AEB4C7E17C60D9F1E0EF0092260A4D92A2079845B1D204780F0D50E64A16`

**Browser response sequence:**

1. It adopts Values as non-negotiable commitments Spark will incur cost to preserve and Principles as decision rules that operationalize those commitments.
2. It replaces “knowledge cannot be stored” with a distinction between repository knowledge representations and human capability.
3. It acknowledges that Knowledge, Understanding, Judgment, and Competence had been collapsed and should be explored distinctly.
4. It accepts that application demonstrates competence rather than propositional truth.
5. It introduces factual/declarative, procedural, and possible conceptual knowledge.
6. It concludes that knowledge can exist without immediate application.
7. It describes Spark OS as a Knowledge Operating System broader than an information or traditional knowledge base, combining repository content, Brain, governance, workflows, learning, judgment, and capability development.

**Audit note:** This is strong progress and resolves several earlier concerns. The revised Value and Principle boundaries now align with the repository. Knowledge representations versus human capability is a productive distinction. Remaining work should clarify whether conceptual knowledge duplicates Understanding, whether a person can possess knowledge without capability, and whether the repository is best described as the authoritative knowledge substrate inside the broader Spark OS. The conversation should now deepen those definitions rather than moving to the next concept too quickly.

### S-009 — 2026-07-21 — Sheldon Phillips and browser conversation

**Sheldon's prompt to the browser chat:** Sheldon asks for the Knowledge, Understanding, Judgment, and Competence model to be tested against misinformation, stale facts, tacit expertise, disputed claims, and correct opaque AI outputs. Sheldon also corrects the vertical component stack, requires ontology work to remain proportionate, and advances five humility boundaries for consideration in the Manifesto and Principles.

**Pasted exchange SHA-256:** `DBC7D3AA3D996E31B8381D59D07FFED5CC7C8811DB3041DE2BA5F7B9699C6D39`

**Browser response sequence:**

1. It reframes the four concepts as related dimensions rather than a universal ladder.
2. The misinformation test separates false belief, coherent explanation, judgment quality, reliable procedure, and truth.
3. The stale-fact test separates historical accuracy from current applicability and proposes provenance and status controls.
4. The tacit-expertise test recognizes partly inarticulable knowledge, judgment, and competence while preserving fallibility.
5. The disputed-claim test preserves uncertainty while allowing understanding, judgment, and provisional action.
6. The opaque-AI test distinguishes output reliability, partial human understanding, human judgment, system performance, human competence, and organizational governance.
7. It refines working definitions and replaces the Knowledge Ladder with an iterative four-concept model.
8. It corrects the repository-versus-system model in prose and restates the humility boundaries.
9. It maps candidate contributions across Manifesto, Values, Principles, Mental Models, and Glossary.
10. It concludes that ontology expansion should stop and substantive five-document development should resume.

**Audit note:** This is the first exploration to complete a credible challenge cycle. Its central result is strong: the four concepts are useful distinctions but not a fixed ladder. The response also corrects the repository/system relationship and returns to the five documents. Remaining cautions should travel with the source material: professional Competence may require more than reliable performance; AI reliability is bounded; the proposed epistemic statuses mix several dimensions; and the model should not be named a Capability Model without further justification.

**Candidate structure:** 1,334 words, ten second-level sections, and fifteen uses of the non-canonical spelling `Spark-OS`.

**Candidate Manifesto sequence:**

1. Defines why Spark OS exists for Spark Mortgages.
2. Describes the organization Spark is building.
3. Places institutional knowledge in service of human and organizational capability.
4. Frames technology as supporting rather than replacing human capacities.
5. Preserves human responsibility and proportional oversight.
6. Explains institutional memory and its fallibility.
7. Favors development over permanent dependence.
8. Seeks coherence without rigidity.
9. Presents deliberate improvement and embeds the full working cycle.
10. Concludes with Spark Mortgages becoming an organization worthy of trust.

**Self-review summary:** The browser response judges the scope, purpose, narrative, humility, universality, document boundaries, and downstream implications positively, while noting that proportional oversight needs later development and that the draft remains a candidate pending audit and approval.

**Independent audit note:** The draft is substantively strong and correctly scoped, with particularly effective technology, institutional-memory, and closing sections. It requires revision before acceptance: standardize `Spark OS`; tighten repetition and section count; distinguish repository presence from authority; add organizational responsibility alongside individual accountability; avoid claiming the system develops people or guarantees help; replace “permanent knowledge”; and remove the full operating workflow from the Manifesto. The workflow should be preserved for installation in canonical Governance or Contribution instructions already loaded by Full Architectural Boot, avoiding duplicated substantive methodology inside Brain.
